Two line labels are protocol, not formatting, and each is exported as a constant and pinned by test to the clause that names it: `IN_FLIGHT_LABEL` for the finish-before-start rule, and **`ROADMAP_LABEL` (added 2026-08-15)** for `contract.AUDIT_VS_READY_PREFERENCE`, which reads the ready and priority-1 counts `tasks.TaskRegistry.summary()` renders onto that line. **Task briefs (2026-08-18, `plan-01`):** two further sections make a request answerable on its own, because `implement` now REQUIRES a decomposition and `revise` may reuse the stored one — neither is authorable from an id and a title. `NEXT_READY_LABEL` renders the READY task `TaskRegistry.next_ready()` would pick with its FULL description (verbatim, uncapped — the reviewer cannot open the repo, so a truncated description is one it would silently plan around) and its `effective_approved_paths`, including the always-allowed trackers, or an explicit "cannot be dispatched until it is scoped" when the scope is empty. `IN_REVIEW_LABEL` renders the task under review with its stored `Task.decomposition` verbatim, so a revise can reuse or replace a plan it can actually see; it carries no description (CONTEXT is not chunked, and a review request already holds the diff), and it is skipped when the registry does not know the task (an audit unit) or when it is the same task `next_ready` already rendered. Both are appended STRICTLY after every stamp line — they embed text this package did not author, so ordering keeps a first-match stamp read correct while `contract.verify_review` does the actual refusing (`docs/SECURITY.md` S33). |
| `context.py` | **Naming what is in flight (2026-08-21, `ctx-01`).** The `in_flight` line kept its two integers and gained ONE row per in-progress task under `IN_FLIGHT_TASK_LABEL` (`in_flight_task: <id> — candidate <sha12>, review round <n>, [no ]revise feedback on record`), plus a `MERGE_WINDOW_LABEL` line saying whether the branch head may move and, when it may not, why. Both are FACTS: the scheduling preference stays in `contract.NEXT_WORK_PREFERENCE`, which is unchanged and still inside its 3,700-character ceiling, so CONTEXT never becomes a second instruction channel. The counts alone were not enough to apply the preference they exist for — on 2026-08-21 a fresh-session reviewer read "2 in progress, 2 holding an unpublished candidate", answered `implement` on a third task and called the held candidates "externally blocked"; neither was, and per-task state appeared nowhere in the block (the roadmap line's "29 blocked" two rows above is dependency-blocked ROADMAP tasks, a different population). The rows come from the same `TaskExecution` records `_in_flight_counts` already loaded — no new data source, and explicitly not the dashboard, which reads the loop's own state files one process away. `last_revise_feedback` is rendered as a BOOLEAN and never as text: it is reviewer-authored prose of unbounded length, and the block's line ordering is load-bearing (`docs/SECURITY.md` S33). Unknown stays unknown at both levels — a record that will not load is listed as `unknown` instead of being dropped, and the aggregate still collapses to `None`. The merge-window line is `cli._merge_window_blockers` **called** (deferred import, the idiom `auto_merge`/`merge_sweep` use, handed the orchestrator's own gateway), never a second copy that could disagree with the loop about whether a merge is safe; its advisory NOTES are dropped, a failed check renders `unknown — <why>` and never `open`, and every reason is flattened to one line because git stderr reaches those strings. Both additions are OPTIONAL and independent: no `executions` means no rows, no `config` means no window line, and a five-positional-argument call renders exactly the block it did before. Measured cost for the 2026-08-21 shape (two in-flight tasks, window shut): **437 characters per round**. |
| `packet.py` | The post-commit review packet, rendered ONLY from immutable git objects in `task_base_sha..candidate_sha` (commit list, changed paths with mode/type from both trees, diff stat, full patch) plus one loudly-labelled section carrying the executor's own claims. `task_id`/`branch`/`base_sha`/`candidate_sha` are stamped as literal lines *inside* the hashed body, which is what stops an approval of candidate A being replayed onto candidate B. **Chunked delivery (2026-08-14, pkt-01):** the packet always inlines the WHOLE patch — that string is what `context.report_sha256` covers — and a patch too large for one chat message is DELIVERED as numbered parts instead of being omitted. `plan_chunked_delivery` splits it at `DIFF_INCLUDE_MAX_CHARS` (30,000 — the per-message budget, unchanged and still pinned to the single measured 40,056-character failure), mints part ids that deliberately do NOT contain the request id (every provider answers "did this land?" with a substring search), and returns the abridged verdict message plus the omission notice to fall back to. Refuses — falling back to omission — if the stored patch is not inside the payload, a part id would collide, or the patch needs more than `DIFF_MAX_PARTS` (6). See `docs/AUTOLOOP.md` §5d. **`_format_out_of_scope` (2026-08-05)** is the control that replaced the path-scope park: an `OUT-OF-SCOPE PATHS (n)` section computed HERE from `commit_range_paths` vs `execution.allowed_paths` — not from `TaskExecution.out_of_scope_paths`, whose pre-commit half comes from the executor's report — labelled in its own text as advisory (the paths did NOT stop the commit) and placed above the diff, which is OMITTED (never truncated) over `DIFF_INCLUDE_MAX_CHARS` (30,000, sized from the one measured send failure at 40,056). Since 2026-08-15 the section renders ONLY when a path is actually out of scope — an always-present `(none)` section trains a reviewer to skim past the packet where it matters; the "was it dropped in a refactor?" question it used to answer is answered by `test_the_packet_names_every_out_of_scope_path` instead. See `docs/SECURITY.md` S25's amendment. **`_format_assumptions` (auto-04, 2026-08-16)** appends `TaskExecution.assumptions` INSIDE the executor-report section (not beside the git-read ones, where an unread claim would borrow their authority), labelled a claim again because that list is what a hurried reviewer reads on its own. Not first-time visibility — `report_details` is the whole agent transcript — but the label plus the accumulation, since `report_details` is replaced every round while the record's list is unioned. Owns BOTH size bounds, since the record is deliberately unbounded: `ASSUMPTIONS_MAX_CHARS` (4,000, ~13% of `DIFF_INCLUDE_MAX_CHARS` so the unread section can never crowd out the diff) renders the newest entries that fit and states how many it withheld, and `ASSUMPTION_MAX_CHARS_EACH` (500) shortens one over-long line — appending a marker that names the execution record rather than a bare ellipsis — because entries render newest-first and stop at the first that does not fit, so a single pasted-reasoning line would otherwise render nothing but a withheld count and hide every real disclosure behind it. The record itself stays complete either way. Rendered only when non-empty — deliberately unlike the empty report, which announces itself: "no assumption lines" is not a claim the executor made, and a "none recorded" line would invite reading it as a certification that the task was unambiguous. |
| `browser/chatgpt.py` | `BrowserChatGPT` (the browser LLMConversation): `attach` / `submit` / `reconcile` / `await_response`. **Optimistic rendering is never treated as submission** — confirmation needs an assistant turn or a reconciliation reload; ambiguity returns UNCONFIRMED and never auto-retries. Realistic composer input (focus + keyboard clear + `insert_text` + content verification + Send-enabled wait), no navigation while awaiting, per-stage bounded timeouts, structured secret-free diagnostics. **2026-07-31:** a fourth result, `REJECTED`, when the network observation *disproves* acceptance (persisted history still outranks it, and a 2xx alone still never confirms); `ConversationUnusableError` for the narrow "this chat loaded and is wedged" case that authorizes a rotation — a page that never reached the conversation stays an ordinary `BrowserError`; and `retarget`/`current_url` so a rotation can move the client to a replacement chat. **2026-08-16:** `find_conversation_with` (identify a chat by the request it contains, for when the address bar lags) mounts the virtualized message tail before concluding — ChatGPT paints a window, not a history — and confirms the page it read is the one it asked for. Absence needs TWO proofs: the session reports the list is at its END (the position `scroll_to_end` now returns), and the mounted window then stops changing. Neither alone is sound — the node count is consistent with a whole conversation sliding past a constant-size window, an unchanged window is equally consistent with a gesture that never moved, and being at the end says nothing while an answer is still streaming into it. When it cannot establish all of that, or cannot prove which page it read, it raises `ConversationSearchInconclusive` rather than guessing "absent". **2026-08-18 (brw-12):** `await_response` runs those same two absence proofs (`_rule_out_missing_submission`) from BOTH surfaces the wedged-conversation fault wears — a response-START timeout expiring cleanly, and the incident's actual shape, a mid-await DOM read dying with the lost-session label itself (`_classify_awaiting_read_failure` catches the `SessionLostError`, re-probes through the same session — a fresh read succeeding is the attachability proof — and only then rules). A submission this loop made that is provably NOT in the conversation — tail mounted to its demonstrable end, page attachable and un-throttled at the moment of conclusion — raises `ConversationUnusableError(code="submission_never_appeared")`, the classification the 2026-08-17 incident lacked (a locator timeout on the loop's own sent message read as a lost session and bought ten minutes of 45-second Chrome restarts against a fault no restart could fix). Anything short of that proof keeps its old route: a probe that cannot read the page, a sighted request, or an absence that cannot settle re-raises the original `SessionLostError` onto the restart path (a clean timeout with unproven absence falls back to the ordinary `stage="start"` timeout); a throttle or logout discovered at the conclusion still routes as itself. |
| `browser/playwright_session.py` | The only Playwright code. Lazy import; connects over CDP; never launches a browser or touches login. Also implements the optional **send-observation** capability — a passive `page.on("response")`/`on("requestfailed")` listener over the conversation-send endpoint. It records a status and a path; it issues nothing. **Every call into Playwright is guarded POSITIONALLY, not by type (2026-08-15):** `rewrite_error` turns a driver-channel failure into a plain `Exception`, so `except playwright.sync_api.Error` missed "Connection closed while reading from the driver" and it killed the process instead of routing through `BrowserError`. `connect` and `_call` now convert anything that is not an `AutoloopError` into `SessionLostError`, keeping the original exception's type name in the message. `describe_cdp_endpoint` adds the evidence a park needs to be actionable — endpoint, port, whether `/json/version` answers, and the pids of non-helper Chromes on the profile and on the port — measured at fault time, because `_handle_browser_failure` restarts Chrome before anything is recorded. That evidence is LOCAL (probes dial 127.0.0.1, `ps` lists this machine), so an endpoint this machine cannot speak for — a non-loopback host, or no usable port — reports all four fields `unknown` and points at that host or at the url, never at the local dedicated profile. **`attachable_page_targets(cdp_url)` (2026-08-17, brw-11)** answers the question `/json/version` cannot: how many `type: "page"` targets the endpoint actually has. A closed browser WINDOW leaves Chrome running and that endpoint answering with a valid `webSocketDebuggerUrl` while `/json/list` returns `[]` — Playwright cannot attach at all, and every check built on `/json/version` calls it healthy. **Zero and unmeasurable are different answers**: 0 (the endpoint answered and named no page) is what lets `orchestrator._classify_rate_limit_state` tell a throttled account from an unusable browser and restart for the second; anything unreachable or unparseable is None, because an endpoint answering nothing is the ordinary `BrowserError` path, which diagnoses and restarts itself. Unlike the probes above, this one describes the endpoint it is GIVEN rather than this machine, so a remote CDP url is asked about itself. |
| `browser/observation.py` | **Added 2026-07-31.** The one signal the DOM cannot carry: whether the browser's own send request succeeded. `SendObservation` (path + status + coarse failure — no headers, cookies, bodies or query strings, so a diagnostics dump cannot leak credentials), a narrow send-endpoint allowlist, and `classify_submission`, which folds one Send click's observations into accepted / rejected / **unknown**. Missing or self-contradictory evidence always degrades to unknown: correlation is temporal (bounded by our own click in a single-actor conversation), not by request id, because reading bodies is off-limits. |
| `browser/chrome_restart.py` | **Added 2026-08-16 (brw-07), wired in by brw-08.** The loop's restart path — `python3 -m autoloop.browser.chrome_restart`, the value `config.example.toml` ships for `browser.restart_command` and what `cli._repair_browser` / `orchestrator._attempt_browser_restart` launch after a session loss. Replaced `scripts/restart_autoloop_chrome.sh` (retired; a `.sh` cannot be validated at all, since the validation runner allows only ruff/pytest/python/npm/npx/tsc — which is how a recovery command that reported success while restarting nothing shipped). Stops EVERY process carrying `--user-data-dir=<profile>` (the shell version stopped one pid and relaunched into a survivor that still owned the debug port), polls until nothing holds the port (a kill is not proof it was freed), then launches and requires `webSocketDebuggerUrl` from `/json/version` before reporting success. **The safety bound:** the profile path is matched EXACTLY and the binary name never is — the operator's everyday Chrome runs from the same binary under a different profile. Every listing, signal, probe and launch goes through `ChromeOps`, a REQUIRED argument of `restart()`, so no test can inherit a live default; `restart()` never raises for an ordinary failure (a second exception on top of a browser fault turns recovery into a crash) and its bounds total ≈123s, under the callers' 180s. `playwright_session.describe_cdp_endpoint` reuses its probes and `matches_profile`. **The transition:** the live `.autoloop/config.toml` is not in this repository, so a config still naming the retired script keeps LOADING unchanged — `load_config` refuses nothing, and every command except a browser restart works on an unmigrated deployment. The compatibility boundary is `scripts/restart_autoloop_chrome.sh` itself, kept as a failing tombstone: it restarts nothing, prints the `restart_command = [...]` line to paste on stderr and exits 1 (both callers surface `result.stderr` only on a non-zero exit), instead of the bash exit 127 that deleting it would have produced mid-fault. `git rm` and a load-time refusal both wait for a later cleanup, once live configs are migrated. |
| `browser/session.py`, `browser/selectors.py` | Mockable session protocol; every DOM selector in one dataclass (UI-drift fix point). Send observation, rotation (`retarget`/`current_url`) and `scroll_to_end` are OPTIONAL capabilities probed with `getattr`, so the in-memory fakes and any future adapter stay valid — absent them, behaviour is exactly what it was before 2026-07-31. **`scroll_to_end(selector) -> bool | None` (position added 2026-08-16)**: it paints more of a virtualized list AND reports where it got to — True (the scroll container is at its end, a list too short to scroll included), False (more below), None (unmeasurable). The return value is the point: an unchanged window cannot tell "fully mounted" from "the gesture missed", so an adapter answering None keeps its sightings and can never establish ABSENCE (`BrowserChatGPT._mount_message_tail`). |
| `config_writer.py` | **Added 2026-07-31.** Surgical, atomic rewrite of `[browser].conversation_url` after a rotation, so the next session does not walk back into the chat the loop just escaped. Rewrites one line and copies every other byte (the config is hand-maintained and full of comments); temp-file + `os.replace`; and `assert_untracked` **refuses** a git-tracked path, fail-closed if git cannot be consulted — a loop that can quietly edit tracked files while recovering from a browser fault is worse than a failed heal. |
| `contract.py` | Response contract **v3** (audit/plan/implement/revise/commit/push/commit_and_push/stop — plus `ask_user`, **retired 2026-08-06**: still in the enum and still parsed, never advertised by `CONTRACT_INSTRUCTIONS`, so a reply from a conversation holding the old instructions reaches `policy.retired_decision_verdict`'s `legacy_ask_user_retired` denial instead of a `unknown_decision` contract violation that spends the parse-retry budget without saying the decision is gone. `ACTIVE_DECISIONS` = `Decision` − `RETIRED_DECISIONS`, derived by subtraction so a new member is active unless explicitly retired; `question` is likewise kept as an accepted top-level key for that one legacy shape only — optional there, rejected as `unexpected_field` on every active decision, and deliberately undocumented in the instructions); task-id work authorization; `reviewed` stamp on git approvals; **required non-empty `commit.paths`**) + strict single-envelope parser (one fenced block, or a rendered/plain object with an optional language label; a second object or trailing text is rejected, never resolved by position) + `verify_review` — coded rejects, never guesses. `TaskSpec.approved_paths` (added 2026-07-31, type/shape-validated only — a list of non-empty strings) lets a `plan` declare a task's write-scope up front; NOT required at the protocol level (would be a breaking wire change to every existing `plan`; `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 3) — the real, fail-closed enforcement is downstream in `tasks.py`/`orchestrator.py` instead (see `docs/SECURITY.md` S25). `CONTRACT_INSTRUCTIONS` = the response format plus TWO advisory clauses, each read by the reviewer and enforced by nothing: `NEXT_WORK_PREFERENCE` (finish work already in flight before starting fresh work) and **`AUDIT_VS_READY_PREFERENCE` (added 2026-08-15)** — while the roadmap has READY tasks, `implement` one of them rather than ordering a fresh `audit`, since an audit ADDS findings. It names `implement` alone because that is the one directive the protocol defines for a READY task (`revise` targets already-started work and is phase-gated). `audit` stays correct when nothing is ready, when every ready task is blocked on something *outside* the roadmap — an unmodelled blocker such as an upstream release or an operator decision, never an unmet declared dependency, which would make the task BLOCKED rather than READY — or when the operator asks. Deliberately NOT a `policy.py` refusal (that layer authorizes actions, and a denial would park the loop instead of redirecting it) and deliberately never absolute (an empty queue is exactly when continuous mode needs an audit to find new work). Each clause carries its own character budget test, because the instructions are re-sent every turn. **`Decomposition` + the optional `decomposition` key (plan-01, 2026-08-18):** the plan an `implement` authorizes — approach, expected files, ordered steps — parsed for SHAPE here and REQUIRED by `policy._check_decomposition`, the same layering `TaskSpec.approved_paths` uses and for the same two reasons (requiring it on the wire would be a breaking change, and a missing plan deserves a policy denial rather than a parse-retry). Accepted on `implement`/`revise` only, forbidden elsewhere. A one-step plan is a first-class answer at every layer, including `render()`, which is the ONE text `Task.decomposition` stores and the implementing agent is shown. The steps stay PROSE and nothing dispatches per step: splitting a task is `split-01`'s atomic mechanism, and a machine-actionable step list here would be a second one. The clause cost 140 characters of instructions and was paid for by compressing 168 out of existing prose, so the budget ceiling did not move. |
| `lock.py` | Single-instance lock per state dir: atomic create, pid/host/start/run-id recorded, live-vs-stale distinction, fail-closed, `unlock`-only recovery (refuses live locks), run-id-guarded release. **`exec_handoff` (2026-08-18, loop-02):** the one way a live lock is ever taken over, and it is a handoff rather than a steal. When the loop replaces its own interpreter to run code it just merged, `os.execv` preserves the pid and runs no `finally`, so the lock file is never released — one live pid holds one lock file across the whole replacement, with no instant another process could take it. The successor image would otherwise fail closed on its OWN pid, so `mark_exec_handoff` writes a marker naming this host, pid and run immediately before the exec, and `acquire` adopts only a lock that carries it, names this pid twice (owner + marker), names the run the lock itself records, and has not been adopted already — adoption clears the marker, so it is one-shot. A live lock without a marker, or one whose marker names another pid, host or run, is refused exactly as before. **The marker alone is not evidence, and that is the point of the token** (`EXEC_HANDOFF_TOKEN_ENV = AUTOLOOP_EXEC_HANDOFF_TOKEN`): host, run id and pid are all readable or reproducible from outside, so a stale-but-valid-looking marker plus a reused pid would otherwise be a complete handoff. `mark_exec_handoff` mints 32 random bytes per arming, puts them in the environment FIRST and then in the lock file (so a marker never lands on disk without an inheritable token behind it), and adoption requires the two to match — which only the image `os.execv` produced can manage, since nothing else inherits this process's environment. Adoption consumes the environment token as well as the marker; a malformed one (non-`str`, empty, non-ASCII — `secrets.compare_digest` raises on the last) is a refusal rather than a `TypeError` inside `acquire`, which in production is the successor's first act after the exec. `clear_exec_handoff` disarms BOTH halves when `execv` itself is refused, the environment one unconditionally and first, since it is what every later subprocess would otherwise inherit. Both rewrites are temp-file + `os.replace`, never unlink-then-recreate: an unlink would open precisely the window the continuity claim denies. `started_at` survives adoption (the lock really has been held since then), which also keeps the predates-boot check honest. |
| `lock.py` | Single-instance lock per state dir: atomic create, pid/host/start/run-id recorded, live-vs-stale distinction, fail-closed, `unlock`-only recovery (refuses live locks), run-id-guarded release. |
| `health.py` | The read-only, lock-free verdict `autoloop health` prints and a scheduler acts on (`needs_attention` / exit code). Signals chosen for false-alarm rate: the LOCK (boot-aware `LoopLock.is_live`, never a process-name match), transcript-tail age (never `state.json` mtime), a live subagent as proof of work (suppresses the silence alarm, evaluated first). **Silence is awake minutes, not wall-clock (2026-08-18, hlth-01):** `machine_sleep_in_window` subtracts everything NOT provably awake from the transcript age before the `DEFAULT_SILENCE_MINUTES` threshold is applied. `lock.boot_time_epoch` is the shared boot boundary (kernel wake history describes one boot; time before it is an off machine, not silence), then `sysctl kern.sleeptime`/`kern.waketime` fill in sleep on darwin (the same sysctl-timeval evidence family `lock.boot_time_epoch` reads, deliberately never a monotonic clock: macOS stops it during sleep) and CLOCK_BOOTTIME−CLOCK_MONOTONIC on linux. The darwin sysctls carry only the LAST sleep→wake pair, so a window predating that sleep credits only the tail since the wake as awake — unprovable time is discounted with the sleep, and a stuck verdict is only ever built on proven awake silence (a real hang still surfaces: its proven-awake tail keeps growing). Unavailable wake history — unreadable boot time included — FAILS TOWARD QUIET on that one axis, with the reason in the detail: a false "stuck" (observed 2026-08-05 — 224 minutes of laptop sleep reported as a hung loop) trains the operator to ignore the monitor, while a missed detection is retried minutes later. Do not raise the threshold to accommodate sleep — that only slows the alert for a genuinely hung loop. |
| `health.py` (strand-01, 2026-08-24) | Second job, and the only one that asks about the QUEUE rather than the loop: `stranded_fault_rounds(registry, execution_store, current_task_id)` is THE predicate for "a round the environment destroyed left this task `in_progress` and the loop moved on", returning a `StrandedRound` per task with the fault code and an `obstacle` string that is empty exactly when the record is safe to hand back to scheduling (`candidate_sha` empty AND `review_round == 0`). One predicate, two readers — `check` only REPORTS, `orchestrator._reconcile_stranded_tasks` ACTS — so the monitor can never name a task the loop already requeued. Carried on EVERY verdict via `Health.stranded_tasks` + a detail clause, becoming the `stranded` code only when nothing else needed attention: a stale lock, an open blocker, FAILED, parked and not-running all return earlier, and those are the states a strand co-occurs with. An unreadable task or state file is REPORTED, never read as "nothing is stranded". |
| `manifest.py` | **RETIRED 2026-07-30, kept for its own unit tests only** (docs/SECURITY.md S21/S22) — no production caller. Was: task-owned change manifests, content-hash snapshots before/after each executor run; `verify_commit` refused pre-existing or untouched paths. Two kinds: `executor` (provenance-bound — this half's own tests, and its only callers, are gone) and `adopted` (content-bound by SHA-256, immutable-tree commit via `commit_adopted` — sound, still tested directly, but its only caller, `orchestrator.py`'s `_dispatch_git`, is also gone). See `docs/AUTOLOOP.md` §4. |
| `doctor.py` | Non-destructive preflight: config, state dir, lock, git identity, branch policy, **worker isolation** (throwaway probe repo via the real `WorkerRepoManager`), **controlled hooks directories**, **publisher configuration**, **publisher URL drift** (§4d), CDP, playwright, provider, conversation URL, **active conversation + rotations used/cap + project-URL shape** (2026-07-31 — after a rotation the state, not the config, says which chat is live, and that is the one an operator needs to open), live login/selector check. Never submits. |
| `audit/` (compaction, 2026-08-01) | **Report size is bounded at the source, never by truncation.** `findings.py` adds structural bounds on the free-text fields (evidence 700, impact 400, action 300, whole-finding 2200) derived from measuring a real report — 28 blocks, median 1,464 chars, one at **21,022**, i.e. 32% of all finding bytes in a single item that had prose written into a field specified as "cite locations". A finding that exceeds a bound is neither accepted nor rejected: it is held as `OversizedFinding` with its item dict intact, and the executor runs **exactly one** reshape round asking that agent to re-express its own words. Still oversized, agent failed, or **fewer findings returned than were sent** → `AuditError` park, with the originals preserved verbatim at `oversized_findings.json`. The count-back is what catches the quietest loss: an agent replying `{"findings": []}` would otherwise look like success. `reconcile.py` dedupes by **merging, not discarding** — the previous rule kept the higher-quality instance and threw the other away, losing impacts and acceptance criteria nothing else carried. A fold requires location overlap **and** a substance signal (high Jaccard overlap of impact + proposed_action, excluding evidence): one finding becomes one task, so folding two distinct defects corrupts task identity, and preserving both texts inside it does not repair that. The threshold errs hard toward leaving findings apart — an un-merged duplicate is a visible extra block, a wrongly-merged pair is a silently lost defect. When a fold does fire it unions every unique evidence reference, impact, acceptance criterion, symbol and command, takes the stronger severity/confidence and the **cautious** parallelism answer. `report.py` renders the proposed task graph once, as JSON — the Markdown table carried the identical fields. |
| `audit/` | Phase-3 audit executor: `findings` (strict agent contract), `agents` (read-only headless `claude -p` runner), `reconcile` (dedupe/classify/reject speculation+style), `taskgen` (`au-NNN` proposal graph), `markdown` (Markdown-only gate, one dated report), `report`, `executor`. |
| `audit/executor.py` | **2026-08-19 (port-03), the domain charters can be shipped by the repository under audit.** `parse_charter_domains` / `render_charter_file` / `load_charter_domains` read `[repo].audit_charters_file` (default `docs/audit_charters.toml`) from the root of the checkout THIS call is rooted at — the audit's own worker repo in production — and `DEFAULT_DOMAINS` stays the fallback, unchanged, for a repository that ships none. Resolved per `execute()` call and cached nowhere, so one process auditing two repositories uses each one's charters. Format is one `[[domain]]` table per domain in wave order with exactly `slug`/`title`/`charter`/`model`; the round trip is exact (nothing normalized), `model` is refused outside `haiku`/`sonnet`/`""` so a repo-side file cannot escalate to the lead's tier, and a slug is restricted to `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` because it names the raw-output file. A file that exists but does not parse returns `ExecutionOutcome(status="error")` BEFORE the run dir or any agent exists — never a fallback to the built-ins, which describe the language-learning app and would file a report that reads as complete about the wrong codebase. Raised as an outcome rather than an `AuditError` because `Orchestrator.run`'s handler chain does not catch that type and the process would end on a traceback the reviewer never sees. |
| `config.py`, `config.example.toml` | **2026-08-19 (port-03):** `[repo].audit_charters_file` joins `env_example_file` / `audit_report_glob` under the same rule — repository-relative, no padding, no `..`, no globs, exact `""` as the opt-out — and `DEFAULT_AUDIT_CHARTERS_FILE` is the fourth `[repo]` default whose value is what used to be unavoidable. Says WHERE the repository states something about itself and grants nothing: read-only confinement stays argv-level in `audit/agents.py` and the always-approved trackers stay `tasks.TRACKER_PATHS` (S31). `cli._build_executor` passes it to `AuditExecutor(charters_file=…)`; a config that omits it behaves exactly as before the key existed. |
| `policy.py` | Deterministic safety layer: directive authorization (git gating + task-graph reference checks), git command whitelist (force push structurally impossible), iteration/failure/parse/denial budgets. **`_check_decomposition` (plan-01, 2026-08-18)** is the gate that makes "every task is decomposed and the decomposition approved before any code is written" real: an `implement` or `revise` must carry `contract.Decomposition`, or the task must already hold one approved earlier, or it is denied `decomposition_missing`. `revise` is covered deliberately: it names a task the same way `implement` does and `_check_task_reference` admits one on a task never implemented at all, so exempting it left a route to starting a task with no plan. It still costs no per-round tax — the ordinary revise-after-implement finds the stored plan and passes carrying nothing, and only revise-before-any-plan is refused. It sits HERE rather than in a round of its own (the loop already exchanges `implement` before any agent runs, so the approval is free; a dedicated plan round would add one to every task, a 30-100% tax on the one-to-three rounds tasks actually take), and HERE rather than in the parser (a missing plan is a well-formed directive that is not authorized, and a denial re-prompts with the whole rule under the denial budget instead of spending the much smaller parse-retry budget). Because `_step_executing` authorizes before `_dispatch`, the refusal lands before `mark_in_progress` and before `_open_attempt` — a plan produces no commit, so it must not spend the budget that bounds commit attempts. |
| `git_gateway.py` | The only git runner — argv subprocess (no shell), policy-validated per call, explicit-refspec push. **The legacy `commit()` method is REMOVED** (2026-07-30, S21: it ran plain `git commit`, gated only on manifest provenance — a pre-commit hook could rewrite approved bytes after the check ran). `commit_adopted` (immutable-tree: `write-tree` → verify → `commit-tree` → `update-ref` CAS, refuses when any commit hook is active) is the primitive that closed the same hole correctly, and is kept — sound, but no production caller anymore either (S22). **Produce-then-review path** (the only live commit path): `commit_and_capture` commits with hooks ENABLED and reads the resulting sha honestly from `rev-parse HEAD` (never predicted), leaving review to `range_diff`/`commit_range_paths` against the immutable commit that already exists; `push_exact` publishes only one already-resolved `<sha>:<dest_ref>` refspec (F2/F5-hardened); `worktree_add`/`worktree_remove`/`worktree_prune`/`branch_exists` support `worktree.py`'s `WorktreeManager`, not what production uses (see `worker_env.py`). `fetch_object(source_path, want_sha)` pulls one already-existing object by literal 40-hex id from a local path (policy-whitelisted with F2-style checks); `__init__`'s optional `env: dict | None = None` lets a gateway run under a scrubbed environment instead of silently inheriting the caller's ambient config — see `worker_env.py`. `object_exists(oid)` (rel-01, 2026-08-15) is the fail-closed existence probe: True/False from `cat-file -e`'s exit code only, raising on anything else, because `read_commit` dies identically for a missing object, a corrupt one and a policy refusal — `cli._candidate_is_retired` needs the distinction before it may write a record off. |
| `auto_merge.py` | **Added 2026-08-14.** The last hop: a completed task's published side branch is merged into the base the loop builds against, and the base is PUSHED (an unpushed merge is the same invisibility one level down). Exists because B10 marks a task completed when its candidate is confirmed on its own branch — publication, not integration — and on 2026-08-06 seven completed tasks sat unmerged, including fixes for failures still occurring. Triggered from `orchestrator._auto_merge_after_completion`, the LAST statement of `_dispatch_task_push` (deliberately after `self._store.save(state)`: the gate reads the phase from `state.json` on disk, and any earlier call would read the stale `executing` and defer forever). Gated on `cli._merge_window_blockers` — **called, not reimplemented**, because a drifted second copy is how thirteen tasks get parked at once. Gate shut / remote base moved / dirty checkout → **deferred** to `MergeDeferralStore` (`<state_dir>/merge-deferrals/`, one file per task, idempotent upsert like `BlockerStore.record`) and retried at the top of the next completion — never parked, since the push already landed. Conflicts read `conflicted_paths()` BEFORE `merge --abort` (the abort clears them), then re-verify head + tree; never force, never auto-resolve. A merge exit of 0 is not evidence: head must have moved, must contain the candidate, must still contain the old base, tree clean — only then `push_exact`. Remote divergence is checked before anything mutates the checkout, because the whitelist has no `reset` to unwind with. **A deferral PINS its execution record:** the retry is drained from that record, so `orchestrator._reconcile_published_execution` refuses to retire one while a deferral is outstanding — archiving it would make the next drain skip the task, and skipping clears the deferral. Flag `policy.auto_merge_enabled`, default OFF. Does NOT sweep branches published before it existed — that is `merge_sweep.py`, which calls `AutoMerger.attempt` per branch rather than reimplementing any of this. **A merge can change the code doing the merging (2026-08-18, loop-02):** when a verified merge touches any path under `autoloop/` (tests included — the claim is "any file"), `_note_loop_code_merge` writes ONE `PendingUpgrade` to `.autoloop/pending_upgrade.json` (`UpgradeStore`) naming the base sha the process would have to be running. That is the module's whole contribution: it does not preflight and it does not replace anything — this code runs mid-round, and the decision belongs to `orchestrator.run`'s boundary and `cli._self_upgrade_at_boundary`. Recorded off the VERIFIED merge, before the push, because the push publishes the base and does not decide what code is in this checkout; the `already_merged` branch records nothing, since the invocation that merged it already did. Under the module's existing failure discipline — a git that will not diff or a state dir that will not take the write logs `self_upgrade_error` and offers no restart, because a raise here would report a real, pushed merge as `failed`. A docs-only merge is silent. See `docs/AUTOLOOP.md` §3f-quater. |
| `auto_merge.py` | **2026-08-19 (docs-01), the one auto-resolved conflict shape.** `_merge`'s conflict branch now offers the conflicts to `_resolve_note_conflicts` before aborting: if every conflicted path is one of the two change-note trackers AND `note_merge.resolve_note_append` accepts all of them, the combined text is written, staged and committed as the merge, logged `auto_merge_notes_resolved`. Every refusal is logged `auto_merge_notes_refused` with its reason and falls through to exactly the abort this path always did — nothing else about conflict handling changed. |
| `note_merge.py` | **Added 2026-08-19 (docs-01).** Pure, git-free resolver for the ONE conflict shape the loop may settle without a human: two branches each appending their own change-note lines to the append-only change-note section that `docs/SUMMARY.md` and `docs/TESTS.md` end with — the one opened by each tracker's `CHANGE-NOTES` comment, which is why prose in these two files refers to that comment by name and never writes it out in full: the resolver requires the marker to appear EXACTLY ONCE per tracker, so a second copy of it anywhere in the file silently switches this whole path off. Takes the three index stages plus git's own half-merged working file; keeps git's merge of everything ABOVE the marker verbatim and rebuilds only the tail, so a conflict in the prose (markers before the marker), an edited/deleted/reordered existing note line (the base section is no longer a literal prefix of a side), a path outside the two trackers, or an appended heading all return `None` and leave the conflict standing. Replaced `merge=union`, which could not be scoped to a region and therefore disabled conflict detection for the whole file. |
| `git_gateway.py` | **2026-08-19 (docs-01):** three thin primitives for the resolver above — `merge_stage_blob(path, stage)` (`cat-file blob :<stage>:<path>`, so the sides come from the index rather than from parsing conflict markers), `add_paths` (`git add -- <paths>`, no index verification: the rest of the index is git's own merge result) and `commit_staged` (`git commit -m`, which records the second parent itself while `MERGE_HEAD` exists). No policy-whitelist change was needed for any of them. |
| `merge_sweep.py` | **Added 2026-08-15 (merge-03).** The other half of integration: branches nobody is going to report. `auto_merge.py` reacts to a completion it SEES; a branch published before it existed — or by a process that died first — has no event left to react to. On 2026-08-06 seven completed tasks were published and unmerged at once (auto-08, auto-12, brw-01, brw-07, inbox-09, rt-10, rt-11) with the base still at d2d4d6b; a hand-written `git ls-remote` loop was what noticed, and two of the seven fixed failures that were still happening. `BacklogSweeper.sweep()` enumerates every COMPLETED task whose execution record carries a candidate, drops the ones already in the base, confirms publication against the remote (`cli._candidate_publication`, so an `ls-remote` equal to the candidate is the only evidence), and hands what is left to `AutoMerger.attempt` — the gate, the dirty check, the moved-base check, the four-part merge verification, the conflict abort and the push are all CALLED, not reimplemented. Three things it adds: **enumeration**; **order** (oldest publication first — `published_at`, with a record that has none sorting ahead of every record that has one, since the field only exists from 2026-08-15, tie-broken on the candidate's committer date — because attempting a branch before the one it builds on manufactures conflicts that do not really exist); and **stopping** (the first branch that does not land — conflict, unverified merge, or deferral — halts the whole sweep, leaves the rest untouched and NAMES them, because a half-swept backlog with one branch aborted mid-way is harder to reason about than a clean stop). Merged-ness is `merge-base --is-ancestor` and NEVER the task status or a branch-name match — those are exactly what made the backlog invisible. The gate is checked ONCE, before the first merge, so a shut window defers the whole sweep instead of writing one deferral per branch. **No state of its own**: the work-list is re-derived from ancestry every run, so a sweep that stopped halfway just re-enumerates what is left. **"Could not look" is never reported as "nothing to merge":** a completed task has three answers, not two — in the base, outstanding, or unjudgeable — and all four unjudgeable states go into `SweepResult.unresolved`, are named (`merge_sweep_unresolved`), are never attempted, and make `is_clear` false so `merge-backlog` exits 1 and the startup hook prints. **Any one of them also withholds the WHOLE invocation** (`HELD`, `merge_sweep_held`): this module deliberately supports a later branch being cut from an earlier one, so merging a candidate the remote DOES confirm can carry an unconfirmed ancestor into the base transitively — publish A, cut B from A, lose A's ref, and merging B alone makes A an ancestor of HEAD anyway. Excluding only the descendants of an unresolved task needs the ancestry of a commit the sweep may be unable to name or resolve at all, so the invariant is the coarse one: nothing has been mutated at that point, so a hold costs a delay and never a bad base — at the accepted price that one stale unjudgeable task blocks every sweep until an operator acts. Startup still only reports and the loop starts normally. Those states: the remote does not confirm the branch (deleted, force-moved, or unreachable — `_candidate_publication` deliberately cannot tell them apart); the execution record cannot be READ; there is no live record and no archived one shows the work already landed; the record names no candidate. An offline run — or one whose only completed task had a torn record — exiting 0 would be the 2026-08-06 invisibility rebuilt inside the tool written to end it. **Retired records are read for one question only:** `retire_execution` archives on publication being CONFIRMED, not merged, so `executions/archive/<task_id>-*.json` is checked for a sha already ancestral to HEAD (silent if so, unresolved if not) — never merged FROM, since `AutoMerger.attempt` reads the live record. **Only the NEWEST retirement answers**, because `archive` keeps one file per retirement (release → retry → the `published-<sha>` retirement that completed the task) and those generations describe different commits, so "any archived copy names an ancestor" clears a task on the strength of a superseded attempt while its completing publication sits unmerged. Generation comes from the archive FILENAME — `retire_execution` appends a fixed-width `YYYYMMDDTHHMMSSZ` instant to every label, and while whole labels do not order across differing reasons, that trailing component does. One archived copy needs no ordering and is judged directly (which keeps pre-stamp records answerable); from two upwards an unstamped label is unresolved rather than guessed at, and a same-second tie requires all the tied copies. Superseded generations are NOT required to have landed — a released attempt's candidate is usually abandoned — or every retried task would be unresolved forever. The glob matches by filename PREFIX and task ids may contain `-` (`rt-1-*.json` matches `rt-1-b-published-<stamp>.json`), so each copy is checked against the `task_id` it carries and dropped only when it proves it belongs to another task; an unreadable or owner-less copy is kept, since "cannot tell" must not read as "not mine". Ancestry alone decides, exactly as on the live path: requiring the archived record's own `published_sha` would be a stricter rule with a date on it (the field only exists from 2026-08-15), leaving every legacy archive permanently unresolved with its candidate demonstrably merged. That is the one deliberate difference from `merge-window`, which ignores the archive because it asks a different question ("could moving the base strand this?" — a retired record cannot). **Publication is re-confirmed per branch immediately before its own merge:** the candidate's key is evicted from the shared `seen` cache first (`_reconfirm`), so branch N is merged on an `ls-remote` taken after branch N-1 landed rather than on enumeration-time evidence, and a ref deleted or force-moved mid-sweep yields `UNCONFIRMED` — that branch and every branch after it are left alone (`merge_sweep_publication_changed`). **A stop is not automatically a restoration:** two `AutoMerger.attempt` outcomes leave the base MOVED — a merge that ran and then failed verification (deliberately not undone; `reset` is off the git whitelist) and one that verified and whose PUSH was then refused, which returns `auto_merge.DEFERRED`, the same slug a shut gate and a dirty checkout produce over a checkout nothing touched. So the claim is never read off the slug: `_probe` observes HEAD and `status --porcelain` immediately before each attempt and again the moment one does not land, and `SweepResult.is_reconciled` is that comparison (an unreadable probe is not a match — "could not look" is not "nothing moved"). Runs at startup (`cli._sweep_backlog_on_startup`, once per process inside the loop lock) and on demand (`python -m autoloop merge-backlog`, which also takes the lock — unlike `merge-window` it moves the head). Behind the same `policy.auto_merge_enabled` flag, default OFF, and every failure swallows to the transcript: a sweep must never stop a run from starting — with exactly one exception, an UNRECONCILED checkout, where `_cmd_run` returns 1 without entering `_run_locked`, since dispatching roadmap work onto a head nobody verified (or pushing work stacked on a merge the remote has never seen) is the very thing stopping the sweep exists to prevent, and there is no policy-legal automatic undo. |
| `merge_sweep.py` | **2026-08-27 (witness-01), the archive-generation reading extracted so a second reader cannot grow its own copy.** `execution_record_ancestry(executions_dir, task_id, ancestry, base_label=…)` + `RecordAncestry` + `RECORD_VERDICTS` are the whole of what `_retired_publication_is_integrated` used to hold: live record if there is one, else the NEWEST archived generation (ordered by the filename stamp, foreign copies dropped by the `task_id` they carry, an unorderable or torn archive refused rather than guessed at), with `ancestry(sha) -> yes/no/unknown` INJECTED so the function reads files and asks git nothing. Four verdicts, and `absent` (nothing names a candidate — including a record that names neither sha) is deliberately distinct from `unverified` (a record exists and could not be judged). `_retired_publication_is_integrated` is now a wrapper that collapses that to the sweep's `(bool, why)` and passes `head[:12]` as the label, so every `unresolved` reason it has ever written is byte-identical; the sweep's ancestry callable never answers `unknown`, so its verdict set is unchanged. Second caller: `dashboard.registry_disagreements`. An id no `TaskRegistry` could issue (empty, containing a separator, or leading `.`) is refused before either read, because both build a path out of it and the page's roadmap read is tolerant. **What an `in-base` verdict proves is bounded and the docstring says so**: the BRANCH is accounted for in the base, not that the task's content survived — a `git merge -s ours` supersede is a genuine ancestor that took no content. |
| `worktask.py` | Produce-then-review bookkeeping (Autoloop M1 pass 1): `TaskExecution` (per-task branch/worktree/base-sha/candidate-sha/review-round record), `CommitIntent` (durable pre-commit marker), `TaskExecutionStore`/`IntentStore` (crash-safe JSON, corrupt records raise rather than reading as absent), `reconcile_after_crash` — classifies a post-crash branch tip as `NO_COMMIT`/`RECOVERABLE`/`AMBIGUOUS` from parent linkage + changed-path subset alone (never identity or commit message, which a human commit in the same worktree could coincidentally match — F8). **Record RETIREMENT (rel-01, 2026-08-15):** `TaskExecutionStore.archive(task_id, label)` MOVES a record into `executions/archive/<task_id>-<label>.json` — never `clear()`, since its `candidate_sha` names a commit that may still be recoverable — and `retire_execution(task_id, store, worker_repos, reason=…)` retires BOTH halves of an execution, record and worker repo, under ONE label so they cannot drift apart. The `archive/` subdirectory is load-bearing: `cli._merge_window_blockers` and `dashboard.merge_states` both glob `executions/*.json`, which does not recurse, so filing a record one level down is what actually takes it out of the merge-window gate. Record first, worker second — a left-behind record is silent (it holds the window shut), a left-behind worker is loud (the next `create()` refuses the directory). Called by `cli._cmd_release` and by `orchestrator._reconcile_published_execution` — the latter first checks the auto-merge deferral queue and PINS a record an undrained retry still reads from, since `AutoMerger.attempt` loads the candidate back off the live file and a skipped drain clears the deferral with it. `TaskExecution` also gained `published_sha`/`published_at`, written by `_dispatch_task_push` from the remote's own answer, never from anything the record claims. **`TaskExecution.assumptions` + `accumulate_assumptions` (auto-04, 2026-08-16):** the durable half of retiring `ask_user` — the readings the executor CHOSE where the task did not say, since an ambiguous task can no longer stop the run to ask one. Accumulated across rounds (union, first-seen order, blanks dropped) rather than replaced, because a round-2 executor assuming nothing must not erase what round 1 assumed and shipped into the range being authorized — and because `report_details`, where the same lines also appear, IS replaced every round; persisted UNSORTED (unlike `allowed_paths`/`out_of_scope_paths` — order is which round chose what) and loaded with a `.get` default, so a record written before the field existed reads as "none recorded". The record is COMPLETE — every line the executor wrote, at full length — and the bounds live where the constraint actually is, at render time (`packet.ASSUMPTIONS_MAX_CHARS` for the section, `packet.ASSUMPTION_MAX_CHARS_EACH` for one line). The per-round caps this shipped with (`MAX_ASSUMPTIONS_PER_ROUND` 20 / `MAX_ASSUMPTION_CHARS` 500) were REMOVED in review the same day: they bounded a chat message by editing a durable record, and since `report_details` is replaced every round, a line dropped there was unrecoverable as soon as the next round committed — the exact cross-round persistence this field exists to provide. `policy.max_review_rounds` defaults to unlimited, so the packet is where ~200 KB of accumulated prose has to be cut (its oversize fallback drops the DIFF, not this section), and truncating the RECORD instead would delete evidence out of the file crash-recovery adoption reads. CLAIMS throughout: like `report_summary`, nothing computes with them. **Two attempt budgets + the attempt ledger (budget-01, 2026-08-17):** `attempt_count` was one counter paying for two unrelated things — a task's own unproductive churn AND rounds destroyed by a provider 429, a stall-killed agent or a process that died mid-round — so six tasks (brw-09, exec-01, port-01, brw-11, dash-04, hlth-01) hit `MAX_TASK_ATTEMPTS` on rounds no reviewer had seen and were repaired by hand. `attempt_count` now keeps only the task's own work (validation failures, structural refusals, rounds that reached the reviewer); `fault_attempt_count` takes the rest under its own ceiling (`MAX_TASK_FAULT_ATTEMPTS`, park code `fault_attempt_ceiling`), so BOTH kinds of churn stay bounded and neither spends the other. `attempt_ledger` is one preformatted `"<ordinal>|<budget>|<reason>"` string per dispatch (`format_attempt`/`split_attempt`) — a string rather than a nested dataclass because `load` does `TaskExecution(**data)` and would hand back dicts, and unsorted for the same reason `assumptions` is. Budgets come in two groups: OPEN (`ATTEMPT_PENDING` = charged to `attempt_count`, `ATTEMPT_PENDING_FAULT` = charged to `fault_attempt_count`) and SETTLED (`ATTEMPT_TASK`/`ATTEMPT_FAULT`). An OPEN label is written at dispatch and replaced at the round's exit, so an entry still reading either one can ONLY mean the process did not survive that round; that is what lets restart reconciliation move interrupted rounds onto the fault budget without ever being able to relabel a finished task failure. There are TWO open labels rather than one because a single field cannot answer both "which counter is this charged to" and "has the round finished" — writing a fault-forced redo straight to `ATTEMPT_FAULT` at dispatch (the first cut of this) erased the second answer, so a redo that reached the reviewer looked like a round with nothing in flight and the NEXT session-ending fault silently charged its redo to `attempt_count`. `reason` is a bare outcome slug, or `"<origin>><outcome>"` for a redo (`compose_reason`/`attempt_outcome`), and every reader keys on the outcome. `pending_fault_code` is the one cross-session marker: set when a session-ending fault destroyed a review the task had already earned, consumed once per dispatch — and RE-ARMED from the same origin when that dispatch is itself taken by the environment without reaching a review (`orchestrator._settle_attempt` rule 4), because the review is still lost and the round after it is still recovering it. Re-arming is why a chain of interruptions cannot fall back onto `attempt_count` at its second link, and each carried-forward dispatch still pays a `fault_attempt_count` charge, so the chain ends at `MAX_TASK_FAULT_ATTEMPTS` rather than being a standing exemption; it clears for good the moment a round reaches a reviewer or fails on the task's own merits. |
| `environment.py` | Snapshot/verify pair (Autoloop M1 pass 1) for what a commit or push actually does that isn't the commit itself: active hooks, `core.hooksPath`, every `url.*.insteadOf` rule, and every remote's url/pushurl/push-refspec. `verify_unchanged` catches drift mid-task (e.g. a dependency postinstall script installing a hook) that pre-commit checks alone would miss. |
| `worktree.py` | Per-task worktree lifecycle: `WorktreeManager.create/remove/list_worktrees`. `task_id` doubles as a path component and a git ref component, so it's validated against `[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]*` plus an explicit `..` check before touching either. Relies on git's own refusal to check the same branch out into two worktrees at once as a fail-closed concurrency property, rather than reimplementing it. **Not what production `_build_orchestrator` constructs** (it always uses `worker_env.py`'s `WorkerRepoManager` instead, which does not share `.git` with the main checkout); still directly tested (`test_postcommit_flow.py`, `test_postcommit_primitives.py`) and still a supported `Orchestrator(worktrees=...)` mode. |
| `validation.py` | Shared `SAFE_VALIDATION_BINARIES` allowlist + `run_validation_commands` runner, used by both `audit/executor.py` (pre-commit repo-health summary) and the orchestrator's post-commit re-validation (pass 2a) — one definition so the two call sites can't drift on what's safe to launch. **Owns the validation subprocess's ENVIRONMENT too (2026-07-31):** never inherits `os.environ`, always passes `strip_validation_vars(os.environ)` and overlays a `ValidationEnv` when one is configured, then redacts its values out of the returned summary (which becomes `state.last_validation` → state.json, transcript, blockers, review packet). Note `AuditExecutor._run_validation` is a SEPARATE runner sharing only the binary allowlist — it deliberately gets no credentials. **Owns the pytest FLAGS a validation run needs too (val-01, 2026-08-06):** `effective_validation_commands` adds `-n auto` (xdist, one worker per core) and `-p no:cacheprovider` to every configured pytest command before running it — skipping a command that selects the `isolated` marker (it must keep its own process) or that already states a worker count, never touching non-pytest commands, and never changing which tests a command selects. Applied here rather than in the config template because nothing re-reads the template: a `.autoloop/config.toml` copied before a flag existed, a task's declared `validation`, and an `execution.validation_commands` record persisted by an earlier session all reach a run through this one function. Pinned by `autoloop/tests/test_validation_parallelism.py`. |
| `validation.py` | **Owns WHICH TESTS a per-commit run executes too (val-02, 2026-08-20).** `select_validation_commands(commands, changed_paths, repo_root, mode, full_reason)` narrows each configured pytest command to the test files reachable from the commit's own changed paths, and returns a `TestSelection` whose `evidence()` is appended to the validation summary — so `state.last_validation`, and with it the CONTEXT block the reviewer reads, states what was selected, from which changed inputs, by what method, why the subset suffices, and whether the run was subset or full. Selection is IMPORT-GRAPH REACHABILITY, deliberately not filename similarity: `build_import_graph` parses every `.py` file with `ast` and reverses the edges, so a change to `publisher.py` selects `test_v1_smoke.py`, which imports it and which auto-01's f06454b5 (2026-08-06) broke while updating only the four test files it touched. Plus two edge kinds no import statement states — ancestor `__init__.py` (importing `a.b` executes `a/__init__.py`) and `conftest.py` (pytest applies one to its whole directory tree). What the graph cannot read is never assumed away: a file using a dynamic import, a TEST spawning an interpreter, or one that fails to parse is `opaque` and sits on EVERY change's frontier; the interpreter signal is test-only, because half this package names `python3` for a living and treating those as opaque drags in everything importing them. Everything else widens to the full list — a non-Python changed path, a path absent from the tree, no changed paths, a checkout over `_GRAPH_MAX_FILES`, a pytest argv with an unrecognised flag or a node-id/glob target (left exactly as configured), and a reachability result of zero test files. Non-pytest commands (`ruff`) are never touched. Three ways to force the whole suite, all deterministic and all OPERATOR edits — a `plan` directive can set none of them, since `contract._TASK_SPEC_KEYS` carries no `validation` key: `[audit] test_selection = "full"`, a task that declares its own `validation`, and a declared `validation_cwd` (paths would not line up). A reviewer who wants a full run has to ask for one; no directive field grants it. Wired at ONE call site — `orchestrator._run_post_commit_validation`, fed `commit_range_paths` from `_verify_committed`, never the executor's own `changed_paths`. The PRE-commit run in `implement_executor.py` is untouched and still runs the full configured list every round. Pinned by `autoloop/tests/test_test_selection.py`. |
| `scripts/seed_validation_db.py` | **Added 2026-08-01** (not under `autoloop/` — it seeds the APP's tables). Deterministic, idempotent synthetic corpus for a validation database: two videos, five sentences, five words + `word_to_sentence` links, all with fixed ids in a reserved `900_000+` range so a second run inserts nothing. Exists because a schema-only database makes ~55 backend tests SKIP themselves ("run the subtitle pipeline first") — and a task graded by a suite that silently opted out of every content-touching test is not validated. Rows are chosen to satisfy specific guards: a lemma with two surface forms in one video, a word whose lemma differs from its surface, a non-ASCII surface, a `word_id` colliding with a real `phrase_id`. **Nothing is copied from any real database**, and it refuses to run against the DB name `.env.example` declares (reusing `validation_env.repo_declared_db_name`). `--verify` re-checks each guard without writing. Deliberately does NOT seed `sich freuen auf` — see its `PHRASE` comment and `docs/AUTOLOOP.md` §4g. |
| `inbox.py` | **Added 2026-08-01.** Operator task inbox: `add-task` submits a request to a directory BESIDE `workers_root` (outside the checkout, so the escape detector never sees it and no lock is needed), and the loop drains it BETWEEN steps, validating through `TaskRegistry.add_many` and writing `tasks.json` itself — the loop stays the only registry writer, with one exception since 2026-08-16 (dash-04): a task's `priority`, which the dashboard writes immediately under the fine-grained mutex. `KIND_PRIORITY` remains drained as before, for a request written into the directory by hand, and takes precedence over the stored value when it is. Exists because editing `.autoloop/tasks.json` directly trips the escape detector (it snapshots ignored paths too) and races the orchestrator's own save; and because `tasks.json` holds `approved_paths`, that snapshot coverage is load-bearing, not a bug to work around. Malformed requests refused at submit; unparseable files moved to `inbox/rejected/`; a request the registry refuses is reported and dropped so one typo cannot stop a run. **Mutation vocabulary, added 2026-08-16 (inbox-02):** `task` + `priority` became `task` + six kinds — `priority`, `description`, `approved_paths`, `depends_on`, `block`, `unblock` — driven off one `MUTATION_PAYLOAD` table that also IS the per-kind "carries only its own field" rule, so a new kind cannot forget it. The allowed-field check is PER KIND in both directions — `_check_mutation` bounds a mutation to `{kind, id, <payload>}` and `_check_creation` bounds a `task` to `CREATION_FIELDS` (`ALLOWED_FIELDS` is now just the union of the two, referenced by `dashboard.TASK_REQUEST_FIELDS` and validated against by nothing). One global set could not express it: `reason` is legal on a `block` and meaningless on a `task`, and checking the union let `{"kind": "task", …, "reason": …}` submit cleanly and then be dropped on merge — the silent ignore the per-kind rule exists to prevent. Two authorities split by question, ONE implementation each: shape is `check_request_shape` (present? right JSON type? does this kind carry it?), content is the registry's, answered by the same validators creation calls — so refusals reach the operator in one authority's words per question. `check_request_shape` runs at BOTH gates, `TaskInbox.submit` AND `apply_requests`, because hand-writing the JSON file is the only operator route to the five new kinds and such a file never passes through `submit`; on merge an `InboxError` becomes that request's `refused` line and the batch continues, so the never-raises promise is unchanged. `apply_requests` applies in submission order in a SINGLE pass — last write wins, and a mutation queued before its target exists is refused rather than deferred — and its middle bucket stays ONE bucket in second position because both drain callers unpack three values positionally and gate `task_store.save()` on `if added or <middle>`. `retire` is deliberately absent: it is written-once with no reverse, which is the unblockable state `block`/`unblock` are shaped to avoid. See `docs/AUTOLOOP.md` §4f-ter and `docs/SECURITY.md` S30. |
| `dashboard.py` | **Added 2026-07-31** — read-only live tracker on localhost (`python -m autoloop.dashboard --repo <checkout> --port 8787`). Exists because the audit fans out subagents whose output lands only when each FINISHES, so from outside a working loop and a wedged loop look identical. Reads `.autoloop/` state, the newest committed `docs/AUDIT_*.md` (the app backlog lives there, not in the registry), and `ps` — `live_agents()` parses `Your domain: <x>.` out of the process table, the one thing not already on disk. **It never writes to the observed checkout**, and every `git` call goes through `--no-optional-locks`: plain `git status` refreshes and rewrites `.git/index`, so a 2s poll was silently dirtying the repo it observes — and a dirty checkout makes the escape detector refuse the next write-capable task. **Visual encoding reworked 2026-08-01 under the dataviz method:** stages are an ordered progression, so exactly one hue marks the stage running now (categorical slot 1), finished stages recede to secondary ink, and `blocked` is the ONLY state allowed a status colour because it is the only genuine health verdict — the previous version painted `active` with status-good green, the "status colour for a non-status series" anti-pattern. Mark hexes were validated by an ad-hoc `validate_palette.js` against the node surface, not the page (light `#2a78d6,#d03b3b` on `#f4f3f0`, dark `#3987e5,#d03b3b` on `#2a2a27`, both ALL PASS; the dark critical WARN at 3.0:1 is relieved by icon+label AND the stage table). **That script was never committed** (verified 2026-08-25: no `validate_palette.js` in any commit on any branch), so it cannot be re-run — what actually guards the palette today is `test_dashboard.py::test_validated_mark_hexes_are_the_ones_the_page_ships`, which pins the hexes but re-checks no contrast ratio. Changing a colour means computing contrast again by hand. **Stale-process banner (2026-08-01):** `PAGE` is baked in at import, so a dashboard started before an edit serves the old HTML for as long as it lives — indistinguishable from a feature that never shipped. The payload carries the import-time source hash and the file's current one; when they differ the page says so. Also: legend, table-view twin, keyboard focus parity with hover, `data-theme` toggle beating the OS setting both ways, proportional figures on stat tiles, and no re-render when the payload is unchanged. **No longer strictly read-only (2026-08-01 priority editing, 2026-08-02 task authoring):** two POST endpoints write to the `TaskInbox` and to nothing else — `/api/priority` (an id + an integer) and `/api/task` (id, title, description, priority and the exact `approved_paths` the operator typed). The inbox lives OUTSIDE the checkout, which is what keeps a submit safe at any instant: a write into `.autoloop/` mid-run is exactly what the escape detector reports, and it would park the loop loop-fatal. Nothing about a path is inferred, no wildcard is representable, and there is deliberately NO path validator here — `TaskRegistry.add_many` stays the single authority on merge, and the endpoint accepts a NARROWER field set than the inbox does (no `validation`/`depends_on`, which are not on the form). The creation form is static markup with its listener bound once, because `render()` rebuilds sections on a 2s poll and would otherwise erase half-typed text and double-submit. Queued requests print their `approved_paths` as text, since the loop merges them without asking again — see `docs/SECURITY.md` S28 for the blast radius, stated rather than implied. **Merged vs unmerged (2026-08-06):** `completed` means PUBLISHED, not integrated, and the gap is where finished work goes missing — seven tasks read done on 2026-08-06 while none of their code was in HEAD, two of them fixing failures that were still happening. `merge_report` shows three states per completed task (merged / published-but-not-merged / not published at all) plus `unknown`, with the not-merged count as a stat tile above the fold. Merged-ness is `is_ancestor` — `git merge-base --is-ancestor <branch sha> HEAD`, exit 0/1 — and NEVER the task status, the execution record or a name match, which are what made this invisible. Two consequences worth knowing: an exit of 128 means the object is absent from the local database, which the observed checkout usually is for a side branch (workers commit in isolated repos), and since every ancestor of local HEAD is present, absent is a definite *not merged* — except in a shallow clone, which is probed and reported unknown; and an unreachable remote or unreadable repo renders `unknown`, never not-merged, so a network hiccup cannot invent an alarming state. The execution record's `candidate_sha` is used as POSITIVE evidence only (that commit is in HEAD → merged even if origin no longer carries the branch); it can never produce a negative. Read-only and lock-free like the rest: `ls-remote` cached 60s per repo, ancestry verdicts cached permanently per `(repo, sha, head)` since they cannot change, `unknown` never cached. **Live progress for the running task (2026-08-14):** `worker_progress(state)` reports insertions/deletions/files-touched and elapsed-since-dispatch for the unit currently executing, so a 25-minute round is legible as "has written 400 lines in 12 minutes" rather than just "an agent is running" (merge-01 was killed by the timeout twice on 2026-08-06 having written 591 insertions across 16 files and then 532 across 15 — that it was working at all was only discoverable afterwards, by hand, in the quarantined worker repo). Read from GIT IN THE WORKER — `git diff --numstat <task_base_sha> --` (so the figure spans committed rounds AND the uncommitted tree; `HEAD` is the fallback and the payload says which was used) plus a capped, symlink-safe scan of `git ls-files -z --others`, because new files are where most of an agent's output lands. Elapsed comes from `state.current_task.started_at`, the dispatch stamp, and only when it names the same task (`TaskExecution` carries no timestamp of its own). **The executor's `report_summary`/`report_details` are never consulted and must never become a fallback.** Three honesty rules hold it up: nothing executing (`state.task_execution` cleared at publish) renders NO figures rather than the last round's; an unreadable worker — or either of the two git reads failing — renders `null`/"unknown" rather than 0, since 0 means "nothing written yet", the alarming state, and inventing it from a directory we could not read would be a lie in the dangerous direction; and a capped scan is marked `partial` and displayed with `≥`. Read-only and lock-free like everything else here (`_run_checked` — the failure-distinguishing sibling of `_run` — injects `--no-optional-locks`), with a 2s subprocess timeout so the page never blocks on git; the figures are excluded from the page's re-render signature and drawn before the unchanged-payload guard, so a ticking clock does not rebuild the DOM every poll. **Roadmap grouped by state (2026-08-15, dash-03):** one flat list of 20+ tasks answers none of the three questions an operator has — what is moving, what needs me, what is next — so `task_groups()` splits it into five counted groups in triage order: In progress / Needs a human / Ready / Blocked / Done. **`TaskRegistry.state_of()` is the ONLY source of that state**, which is a correctness rule and not a style choice: `pending` on disk covers BOTH Ready and Blocked (the difference is derived from dependencies and never stored) and a stored `status="blocked"` means QUARANTINED (`TaskState.BLOCKED_BY_OPERATOR`), so a page reading the status string would be wrong in both directions at once — sending an operator to `autoloop answer` for a task merely queued behind another while one that really needs them reads as queued. Constructing the registry is a pure read (`from_dict` + lookups); no mutating method is reachable and no lock is taken. Per group: In progress carries the execution record's `candidate_sha` + `review_round` (the loop's own bookkeeping, never an agent's claim, and "no candidate committed yet" when it has neither); Needs a human carries `blocked_reason`; Ready is `next_ready()`'s own `(priority, id)` order — repeated rather than simulated, because simulating it means COMPLETING tasks, and pinned against the real `next_ready()`/`mark_completed()` loop by test; Blocked NAMES the incomplete dependencies (blocked without saying by what is not actionable); Done is a collapsed `<details>` with its count in the summary, in STATIC markup so expanding it survives the 2s poll, carrying no priority input (the Save binding is scoped to `#roadmap`) and saying in as many words that done means the reviewed candidate landed on the task's OWN side branch (`docs/AUTOLOOP_TODO.md` B10) — published, NOT merged, which is the merge panel's question. Empty groups are hidden EXCEPT Needs a human, which renders and says `none`: silence and "nothing needs you" must not look identical. The hide rule lives in the payload (`hidden`), not the template, so it is assertable. An empty `groups` list means the graph could not be READ (a roadmap with no tasks still returns five zeroed groups) and the page says which, since "no tasks" and "unreadable" call for opposite reactions. `_executions()` is the shared read of `.autoloop/executions/*.json` for this panel and the merge panel — `glob`, never `rglob`, because a retired record is filed one level down in `archive/` precisely to drop out of it. **Retired group (2026-08-16, task-retire-01):** a sixth group between Blocked and Done, for `TaskState.RETIRED` — work that is neither waiting on a dependency nor waiting on a human, because it already shipped (or stopped) under another id. Six of the seven `blocked` rows on 2026-08-14 were this, which is what made the blocked count worthless as a call to action. Its rows name the SUCCESSOR (`_retired_detail`: `superseded by brw-07, brw-08`, falling back to `blocked_reason` for a task that went stale rather than being replaced, and to a stated "no successor recorded" rather than a blank cell), and `superseded_by` rides on every group's task rows so a consumer of the JSON can follow the chain. Collapsed like Done and for the same reason — it only grows and nobody has to act on it — which is why each disclosure is now looked up by GROUP KEY: `groups.find(g => g.collapsed)` returns the first, and with two collapsed groups it would have quietly filled the Done box with retirements. `collect()` also overlays the registry's derived state onto the flat `roadmap` rows, because the migration in `tasks._migrate_retirements` runs on LOAD while only the loop writes `tasks.json` — without it the app-task panel would mark brw-02 "blocked" two panels below the Retired group listing it. **Audit-report location configurable (2026-08-16, port-02):** `app_tasks(repo, limit, report_glob)` takes the glob instead of hardcoding `docs/AUDIT_*.md`; `collect` supplies `_audit_report_glob(repo)`, which reads `[repo].audit_report_glob` out of the RAW `config.toml` via the new `_config_toml` helper (`_inbox_dir` now shares it) rather than through `load_config`, which is strict and would raise on a checkout that has never been configured — this page must render against any checkout. An absolute, traversing or empty glob yields no rows instead of raising, because `Path.glob` raises outright on an absolute pattern and a read-only tracker has no business reading outside the checkout it was pointed at. **Priority edits are APPLIED, not queued (2026-08-16, dash-04):** `/api/priority` no longer writes an inbox request — it calls `TaskStore.apply_priority` on `.autoloop/tasks.json` and answers with the value READ BACK from the file, which is what proves persistence (echoing the posted number would report success identically whether or not anything landed). Queued was the previous design and it failed the operator: the value became true only on the loop's next drain while the page kept re-rendering the old number from `tasks.json`, so a save that worked was indistinguishable from one that did not. It takes NO `LoopLock` (held for the whole run) — the fine-grained `tasks.task_file_mutex` instead, which the loop's own saves take too — writes nothing else in the state dir, and is bounded to `PRIORITY_REQUEST_FIELDS = {id, priority}` with anything else REFUSED rather than dropped; it cannot create the registry. `_tasks_file`/`_state_dir`/`_task_store` resolve the file through `[paths].state_dir` so the page reads and writes the same path, and the ledger the write is attested into is resolved by `tasks.mutation_ledger_for` from `[paths].workers_root` — the same helper the loop uses. Creation stays queued to the inbox, because a new task carries authorization. See `docs/SECURITY.md` S32. **Throughput summary at the TOP of the page (2026-08-16, dash-05):** `roadmap_stats()` answers the three questions the flat list did not — how much is done, how much is moving, is the queue converging — in a `<section id="summary">` that renders above `#tiles`, the pipeline, the merge panel and the roadmap. Counting it by hand took a script (2026-08-06: 66 tasks, 17 completed, 23 in progress, 18 pending, 8 blocked). It is fed the `groups` payload `collect()` already built, so every TASK-STATE figure comes from `TaskRegistry.state_of()` BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by test — a second `TaskRegistry.from_dict` here could drift from the list rendered under it. `STAT_BUCKETS` is ONE COUNT PER `TaskState`, keyed by `TaskState.value` and pinned to a `GROUPS` key, in `TaskRegistry.summary()`'s own vocabulary — completed / in progress / ready / blocked / quarantined / retired — so the page and the review packet cannot report two roadmaps, and `stats["line"]` is that sentence minus only summary()'s priority-1 breakdown and `next ready:` tail. **Revised 2026-08-16 after review:** the first version rolled READY ∪ BLOCKED into `pending` (their split is derived and never stored, so the stored FILE FORMAT really does conflate them) and spent the freed name on BLOCKED_BY_OPERATOR — which put `blocked` = quarantine at the top of the page and `Blocked` = waiting-on-a-dependency in the Roadmap panel below it, two states under one word with opposite calls to action, which is the exact confusion `TaskState` was split up to end.
